## 2026-09-25 — Old Mill Road, the shipped masteries (issue 69) — Code

Seed: 211   Result: win, rout, turn 8 of 12. Recall spent twice, one left. No deaths in the final line.
Not a Fun Gate entry and not cold: Code picked the nine masteries. The point of the play was to see whether a mastery lands inside one map at 12 combats. It does not: the captain, who fights more than anyone, ends at 9 of 12 (`show` printed 8 of 12 on turn 8, then the bandit's attack made it 9). At the 8 first built, he would have mastered Fundamentals on turn 8's attack, and on the seed-139 replay Wren would have mastered it on enemy phase 6 of 7. So at 12 the mastery is a thing the campaign gives on the second map, which is what the issue asked for, and nothing a single map shows yet.
Tension: 8/10   Choice: 7/10   Surprise: 6/10
Best turn: turn 8, after the second Recall. The keyed rolls made Wren's swings a wall: she missed the road archer at 85 from two tiles and missed the mill bandit at 85 on two different turns, and the second miss killed her on his counter both times. With one Recall left, the bandit on 5 and Wren on 10, `threat wren from 2,2` printed the forest's price, 9 at 54 against her 10. She walked into the trees, the bandit took the fort tile and swung at the captain instead, and the captain's counter finished him.
Notes: Turn 1 cost the captain 15 of 22 to the road pair, and the brigand died on his counter; the road was over by turn 3. The mill was the fight, as both journals of this map say. One readability snag, not filed: on turn 5 `threat wren from 6,3` said no enemy could strike her, and the mill archer then walked out and hit her for 6. The group woke on the captain's move to the fort, after the threat was asked, so the line was true of the board it read; it cannot price a group the same phase is about to wake. Where I stopped caring: nowhere; the map is eight turns and every one after turn 4 had a live roll.
Transcript: docs/transcripts/2026-09-25-old_mill_road-211.txt, with the script beside it; it replays under `--strict` with no rejected lines.

## 2026-09-25 — Old Mill Road with Keziah, gauntlets (issue 70) — Code

Seed: 7   Result: win, rout, turn 7 of 12. No Recall spent, no deaths.
This is #70's content hand play on `docs/samples/old_mill_road_keziah.map`, the shipped map with Keziah (reaver, Iron Axe in slot 1, Iron Gauntlets in slot 2) deployed in Wren's place. Not a Fun Gate entry and not cold: Code picked the numbers and the wielder.
Tension: 6/10   Choice: 5/10   Surprise: 6/10
Best turn: turn 2, the first time the choice was on the screen. From 4,7 the Iron Axe printed 14 at 67 on the 17-HP road archer and the gauntlets printed 9 x2 at 91. Neither kills alone on paper; the gauntlets kill if both land, 83 percent. I took the fists and the first strike crit for 27 at 1 percent. The captain finished the brigand on 8 and the road was over by turn 2.
Notes: The choice was real once and then it was not. After turn 2 I never swung the axe: against the soldier on 13 (7 x2 at 91 against 12 at 67), the archer on 6 and the mill bandit on 14 (8 x2 at 91 against 13 at 67) the gauntlets were the better number every time, because this map's enemies sit at Def 2 to 4 and Keziah has Str 9. That is the break-even the lean predicted (two strikes win while Def is under Str less one), and on Old Mill Road nothing is armoured, so the axe's only job was the turn-1 counter. The equip rule makes the gauntlets her counter weapon too once swung, which is what made the fort at 6,2 a fist trap: the soldier's 8 at 49 bought him 7 x2 at 91 back. The tension was all the captain's: `threat captain from 5,3` said nothing could strike him, then Keziah's move to the fort woke the mill and the archer and the bandit took him to 8 (the same snag the seed-211 entry names). On turn 6 no tile he could reach was outside the bandit's 9 at 83 against his 8, and the dressing (supplies 1) was the only line; he lived on 9. Where I stopped caring: turn 3, walking to the mill. For the Table: whether gauntlets that dominate the axe on soft targets are the trade the issue wanted, or whether the axe needs an enemy it is for. Armour arrives with map 4 onward; this map cannot answer it.
Transcript: docs/transcripts/2026-09-25-old_mill_road_keziah-7.txt, with the script beside it; it replays under `--strict` with no rejected lines.

## 2026-09-25 — Certification trials, Bulwark and Outrider (issue 73, experiment 13.6) — Code

Seeds: Bulwark 11 and 12, Outrider 7. Results: Bulwark seed 11 won by survive and earned the class; seed 12 lost to the gamble. Outrider seed 7 failed on the centre lever, then won by escape on the east lever.
This is the spike's hand play on the two samples under `docs/samples/certification/`, with the captain as candidate. It is not cold: Code built both puzzles and knew the answers. The scores rate the trials, not a map.
Bulwark: Tension 6/10   Choice 7/10   Surprise 5/10
Outrider: Tension 2/10   Choice 4/10   Surprise 4/10
Best turn: Bulwark seed 12, the one I lost. `threat captain from 5,3` had already proved the corridor safe: one brigand's 8 against 25, and no Cinder can reach. Then `forecast captain hexer-1 from 3,1` printed 13 x2 at 88 on a 16-HP hexer, and I took the bet a real player would take. The first strike hit and Cinder came back for 11. The second strike killed. On 14 HP, three brigands came around the wall and the second one finished it. The corridor is the proof and the hexer is the temptation, and the trial teaches the bulwark's Res by letting you pay for ignoring it. That turn is the one thing in the spike I would put in front of a player.
Notes: The Outrider trial is a quiz. Once you know forest costs 3 for a horse, it is one sum: 4 to the east lever, 2 of Canto home. The refusal on the centre lever (`not within the 2 movement its Canto has left from 3,3`) does the sum for you after the fact. There is nothing to be tempted by and nothing to lose but the sum. A version with an enemy in it, where the Canto has to buy a strike and an escape, would be a different trial, closer to what `canto_raid` felt like. Both trials need the console to show their levers, so a certification map prints its events at the start. Where I stopped caring: the Outrider trial, the moment I had counted. For the Table: is a trial a puzzle only when it has a bet, and if so, which classes have one?
Transcripts: docs/transcripts/2026-09-25-bulwark_trial-11.txt, 2026-09-25-bulwark_trial-12-gamble.txt, 2026-09-25-outrider_trial-7.txt and 2026-09-25-outrider_trial-7-centre.txt, each with its script beside it. A test replays all four byte for byte, under `--strict` except the centre line, which ends on the refused Canto. (Issue 252 killed the Outrider sample as authored and removed its two transcripts; the rebuild's are under 2026-09-26.)

## 2026-09-25 — Campaign, Old Mill Road then Saltmarsh Ford (issue 74) — Code

Seed: 139 (map 1 on 139, map 2 on 140). Result: both maps won. Old Mill Road was the known seed-139 line by rout on turn 7. Saltmarsh Ford was new, by rout on turn 9 of 18 with one Recall. Nobody fell in the final line.
This is #74's hand play through the between-map screen, not a Fun Gate entry. Map 1 replays my own seed-139 line, and map 2 is played cold on the roster the screen left me: Pell benched in for Ottilie, and a Field Dressing bought for him.
Tension: 8/10   Choice: 6/10   Surprise: 7/10
Best turn: Saltmarsh turn 8, the Recall. Pell's Cinder took the boss to 16 on his fort, and Wren's 8 x2 at 73 was an exact kill. I took it knowing the counter: 15 at 51. The first strike landed, the counter landed, the second missed, and Wren stood on 7 beside a boss who healed to 13 and killed her on the enemy phase. For the first time a death meant something past the map. She had mastered Cadet two turns earlier on points carried from Old Mill Road, and the record would have lost her for the campaign. I spent the Recall on the state after Pell's cast. Wren stepped back to the bridge. The boss threw at Pell instead, missed, and ate Pell's counter. Next turn Pell and Wren finished him. The rolls did not change, and the choice did.
Notes: The screen is thin, and that is the point. What made it matter was carry-over. Wren's 9 of 12 mastery points from map 1 turned into Fundamentals on map 2, which no single-map play has ever shown. The permadeath check at the end of Saltmarsh made the Recall a real decision, not a reflex. Pell's Gust finished the wingrider from two tiles with no counter (16, effective against fliers). That is the best argument the adept has made in any journal, and it only happened because the bench moved him in. The purse is loose: 1120 with nothing on the shelf worth buying before the Tollgate. A shop means little until there is something to want, so steel is the first lever. One `threat` snag, already known: `threat pell` on turn 5 priced only the archer, and soldier-2 then crossed the bridge the archer had vacated and swung at Pell (11 at 74, missed). That is the phase-start caveat in section 5, and it was the tensest roll of the map. Where I stopped caring: the screen before map 2 once the bench was set, since there was nothing to buy that I needed.
Transcript: docs/transcripts/2026-09-25-campaign-139.txt, with the script beside it; a test replays it byte for byte. Two lines are refused on purpose (benching Wren, whom Old Mill Road names, and repairing Pell's Cinder), so it does not run under `--strict`.

## 2026-09-26 — Harrow Weir, map 4 (issue 78) — Code

Seed: 7   Result: win, defeat_boss, turn 7 of 14. No Recall spent; Teodor dead.
This is the builder's play, so it is not cold: I drew the bridge and chose the plug. Chat's cold play is the first real Fun Gate entry for this map.
Tension: 7/10   Choice: 7/10   Surprise: 7/10
Best turn: enemy phase 6. The foreman was on 7 of 28 after Pell's cast and Teodor's lance, so the map was one hit from over. Pell was on 3 HP at 11,6 with the Toll Axe able to reach her, and I was sure he would throw at the kill. Instead he punched Teodor beside him and critted for 24 at 2 percent. The forecast said 8 x2 at 88, and no Recall was going to change a keyed roll. Pell finished him on turn 7 from 3 HP.
Notes: The plug is the map's decision, and it read as one on turn 2. Wren had come out of turn 1 on 9 HP after killing the wood brigand on the counter. Sending her north to stand on 7,0 turned her low HP into an asset, since nothing arrives on a plugged tile. It also cost the bridge a sword for the whole map. The announcement did its job, because I counted the walk, 11 tiles and three phases, before I moved. The weak spot is `threat`. From 9,6 on turn 3 it said no enemy could strike Pell, with a rider due that same enemy phase. That is #248's shape with a spawn in place of a sleeping group. Before the Toll Axe was added, the boss was a free kill from 11,6. With it, 11,6 is the map's second decision, and it cost Pell 13 on her first cast. The bridge fell on turn 3 to Pell's 13 and a Teodor crit, which is faster than it should be. On a seed without the crit, the wall would have held another turn with the ford group arriving. The west brigand arrived on turn 5 and never mattered. Having a wave that the plug cannot reach is right, but this one lands too far from the fight to count.
Transcript: docs/transcripts/2026-09-26-harrow_weir-7.txt, with the script beside it; a test replays it byte for byte under `--strict`.

## 2026-09-26 — Harrow Weir, map 4 (issue 78) — Chat

Seed: 41   Result: win, defeat_boss, turn 8 of 14. Two Recalls spent, nobody dead at the end.
Cold play: Chat had not read 0052, the PR, the map file or Code's entry.
Tension: 7/10   Choice: 5/10   Surprise: 6/10
Best turn: the second attempt at player phase 7. The first attempt went wrong. I threw Pell, Dunstan and the captain at the foreman on the hill, Dunstan and the captain both missed, and the enemy phase killed Teodor at the rear and Dunstan at the boss. I recalled to the turn start, and the console told me that "the same attack will roll the same". So I knew which attacks would land. Pell's 12 and Wren's 7 were real and Dunstan's swing was not. I rebuilt the turn around the two hits I trusted. I kept Dunstan out of the foreman's axe range and sent the captain back to kill the west brigand, which he did with a crit after taking a 10. That left the foreman on 9 for Wren's counter. He missed her twice, she took him to 2, and she finished him on turn 8. Recall stopped being an undo and became scouting. That was the best moment of the map, and I am not sure it is a good thing.
Notes: For me the map's decision was the shieldbearer. Pell's 13 from 9,6 and one melee body on 10,6 was the only way through. Pell plus a full captain round came to 23 against 24 HP, one short, and that felt authored in the good way. The captain's counter finished the job on enemy phase 4. The ford waking on the bridge's noise was the map's real surprise. Then the brawler walked through the ford into Pell and Dunstan and died on turn 5 without ever throwing a punch. The archer at 14,4 waited on all seven enemy phases. The west brigand did matter. It caught my stragglers, and in the first attempt at turn 7 it killed Teodor. I never plugged 7,0. It did not occur to me that I could: the announcement printed the file's syntax, and nothing on screen said a held tile spends the event (issue 256).
Transcript: docs/transcripts/2026-09-26-harrow_weir-41.txt, with the script beside it; Code replayed it under `--strict` to the same game.

## 2026-09-26 — Sallow Grange, map 5 (issue 79) — Code

Seed: 23   Result: win, seize, turn 8 of 8. One Recall spent (turn 6 back to turn 2); nobody dead.
This is the builder's play, so it is not cold: I drew the gates and knew the lock needed Pell. Chat's cold play is the first real Fun Gate entry for this map.
Tension: 8/10   Choice: 7/10   Surprise: 7/10
Best turn: turn 6, second pass. The field had woken on turn 5, the gate shieldbearer had died on Wren's counter, and the captain had two turns to cover nine tiles through a yard with a hexer in it. Ansgar went through the gate, killed the hexer from 13,3 at 83 percent twice, and ate 12 on the counter to stay on 8. That opened 14,6, the one tile four short of the throne and three from the Reeve's spear. The captain stood there on enemy phase 7 while the soldier chased Ottilie to 11,1 and missed at 70.
Notes: The long way is a clock, and I lost to it the first time. On turn 6 of the first pass the gate was still full, and Pell was a turn late because I had walked her along row 1 instead of through 5,2, the one tile beside the fort archer that is out of his ring and out of the field's. The map punished a single tile of routing by a whole turn. I spent the Recall on turn 2 and it was the right spend. On the second pass I then made the map's other mistake: Pell cast at the gate from 10,2, which is 6 from the field archer, and the field woke on noise. I had counted the gate tile and not the caster's tile. That is the surprise, and it is fair, since the rule is on the screen. The long way turned into a fight at its end, which is better than a walk. The fort was never worth taking. The Reeve never swung, because the captain never stood in his ring, so he scored as scenery, the same as the Tollgate's boss. What I did not test is the short way by hand. If it wins comfortably for a human, the long way is only for players who read the wake radius, and that may be right.
Transcript: docs/transcripts/2026-09-26-sallow_grange-23.txt, with the script beside it; a test replays it byte for byte under `--strict`.

## 2026-09-26 — Brackwater Cut, map 6 (issue 80) — Code

Seed: 29   Result: win, escape, turn 8 of 8. Two Recalls spent (turn 4 back to 3, turn 6 back to 5). The captain walked out alone; Dunstan, Rook, Wren and Pell all fell in the line that finished.
This is the builder's play, so it is not cold. I drew the wall and knew where the hexer was. Chat's cold play is the first real Fun Gate entry for this map.
Tension: 8/10   Choice: 6/10   Surprise: 7/10
Best turn: turn 3 of the second pass. I moved Dunstan off the gap and onto 12,3, one tile east, only because 11,3 had cost me three units the first time. On enemy phase 3 the archer took 10,3 and the brigand took 11,3, and everything else behind them stood still. The hexer that had killed the first pass had no tile to cast from. The wall had become one melee tile and one bow tile, and the pursuers had plugged their own corridor for me. That was the moment the map worked.
Notes: The first pass is the issue's warning come true. I held 11,3 the way the brief describes it, woke the bank early with Wren on a forest, and on turn 4 the hexer was in range. Dunstan either left or died, and he did both, along with Rook to the gauntlet brawler (10 twice) and Wren to the shieldbearer. "Do I pull the blocker out now" was the tense question, and the hexer answered it on turn 4 whatever I wanted. On the second pass I lost Rook to a bow from 10,4 while he stood on 12,4. That tile is east of the wall and still two from the west side, and I had not counted it. The wall is one tile thick, and I drew it that way and still forgot. Pell died to the brawler on 13,2 on both later passes. The bank fights better than the pursuers. By turn 8, the only way the captain's walk to 19,3 could count was for Dunstan, at 2 HP and too slow to reach an exit, to die first, and he did, on enemy phase 7. By the rule that is a win. It did not feel like one. What I am unsure of is whether the map is too hard for a party that means to keep its recruits. The heuristic never does keep them: every one of its wins is the captain alone (#263). Nobody has yet escaped this map with the party.
Transcript: docs/transcripts/2026-09-26-brackwater_cut-29.txt, with the script beside it; a test replays it byte for byte under `--strict`.

## 2026-09-26 — Brackwater Cut, map 6 (issue 80) — Chat

Seed: 73   Result: win, escape, turn 7 of 8. Four of five out. Dunstan died holding 12,3. One Recall spent.
Cold play: yes, except that on turn 1 Chat read the map file's `exit:` line, because nothing on screen says where the exits are (#268).
Tension: 8/10   Choice: 7/10   Surprise: 7/10
Best turn: player phase 6. Turn 4's noise had woken the bank, and it walked four tiles north to meet the party, leaving the south exits empty. The captain killed the soldier with exactly 9. Wren's two 3s and Pell's 13 took the shieldbearer from 17 to 0. Then Dunstan, on 12,3 at 2 HP, swung at the rider sitting on the cut with 1 HP: 73 to hit, and 82 that the counter kills him if he misses. He hit. The archer behind the rider shot him dead in the enemy phase anyway, and the other four walked out on the next turn. The best single turn on any map so far: everyone had a job, and the roll that mattered was the rearguard's.
Notes: 12,3 found on turn 2 from the geometry alone. From turn 3 to turn 6, five pursuers queued behind whichever of them held 11,3, and the hexer never reached Dunstan because the chase's own bodies blocked it. On the first turn 3, archer-1 shot Rook over the wall from 10,5 to 12,5 for 19, and the combat woke the bank; `threat` would have shown it, so Chat recalled. On turn 4, Pell's kill from 13,3 was 6 from the shieldbearer and woke the bank: the plug's own fight at 12,3 is 7 away and silent, but anyone who helps the plug from 13,3 or 12,4 is loud, so the holder fights alone (named intended in 0054). The rule makes the rearguard an obstacle whose death wins the map; Chat's lean to escape by leaving is #269.
Transcript: docs/transcripts/2026-09-26-brackwater_cut-73.txt, with the script beside it; Code replayed it under `--strict` to the same game.

## 2026-09-26 — Sallow Grange after issue 259 (the Reeve wakes, clock 10) — Code

Seed: 31   Result: win, seize, turn 7 of 10. No Recall, nobody dead.

Tension: 6/10   Choice: 7/10   Surprise: 6/10

## 2026-09-26 — Brackwater Cut under escape by leaving (issue 269) — Code

Seed: 73   Result: win, escape, turn 7 of 8. Escaped: Rook, Wren, Pell, the captain, in that order. Dunstan fell holding 12,3. One Recall spent (Chat's).

Tension: 6/10   Choice: 6/10   Surprise: 5/10
Best turn: player phase 6, and the best part was the thing I did first. Rook was on 19,6 at 6 HP. Under the old rule he stood there, and the only reason he lived was that the bank was already dying. Now he leaves through the exit before anything else happens. That was the first time on this map that a unit reaching the exits felt like getting a person out, not like parking one. After that I played Chat's turn: the captain's 9 on the soldier, Wren and Pell on the shieldbearer, and Dunstan's 73 on the rider. That roll hit again, because rolls are keyed.

The choice the rule creates, leaving now or holding a phase for the rearguard, would have been real on turn 7 if he had lived. Here the enemy took it away first. So this replay shows the killing floor gone, but not the rearguard decision. That needs a seed where the holder comes out of turn 6 alive, and it needs a cold play, which is Chat's. Turn 7 was bookkeeping: three moves and three exits, the captain last. The summary line made the price visible: "escaped: rook, wren, pell, captain; left behind: none; fell: dunstan".

## 2026-09-26 — Sallow Grange the long way, hexer at 13,7 (issue 275) — Code

Seed: 61   Result: win, seize, turn 10 of 10. Nobody fell. Three Recalls, all on turn 4.

What wasn't tense: turns 8 to 10 are a walk with nothing on the board able to touch anyone. The price of the long way is the clock and the fort archer, not a fight at the end. ## 2026-09-26 — The Outrider trial rebuilt with a guard (issue 252) — Code

Seeds: 12 (won), 13 (lost), 13 flank line (refused). The candidate was the captain, and it ran from `content/trials/outrider_trial.map`. The campaign screen played the same trial with `trial captain outrider` on campaign seeds 6 and 7, which are trial seeds 12 and 13.
Not cold: I built it and knew the answer. The scores rate the trial, not a map.
Tension: 6/10   Choice: 6/10   Surprise: 5/10
Best turn: seed 13, the one I lost. The forest at 3,3, straight in front of the hexer, is the only tile the guard can be struck from with 3 Canto left: 3 for the forest, then 3,2, 3,1 and the throne at 3,0. Either flank costs 5 through the forest band and leaves 1. I read the forecast from 3,3: the lance is 14 x2 at 88 and the sword is 13 x2 at 92. Against 16 HP both need two hits, so I took the sword. The first swing missed, the hexer's Cinder came back for 10, the second swing hit, and the guard stood on 3 HP. With the gate still held there was nowhere to ride, so I stayed, ended, and lost. It was one roll at 85 percent, the price was the whole attempt, and the screen said the trial opens again after the next map. That part works: a failed trial costs a camp, not a unit.
Notes: The old trial was a sum. This one is a sum and then a roll, so the Canto buys the strike and the escape. The forest tile looks like the worst place to fight from and is the only right one. It also takes the counter's hit from 98 to 81, which I did not plan and like. The flank line (seed 13, 2,2) shows the lesson the hard way: kill or not, the Canto has 1 left. The refusal names only the movement left. When the guard lives, it should say the guard still holds the gate. What is still missing is a bet. There is one line and one weapon worth taking, and the lance at 77 is only a worse sword. The rebuild has a proof and a price but no temptation, which is what 0049's lean asks a trial to have. Where I stopped caring: after the forecast. Once the sword was picked, the rest was the dice.
Transcripts: docs/transcripts/2026-09-26-outrider_trial-12.txt, 2026-09-26-outrider_trial-13.txt and 2026-09-26-outrider_trial-13-flank.txt, each with its script beside it. A test replays all three byte for byte.

Not cold, and warmer than usual: this is my second line on this seed. The first line was on a slot order I then dropped (Keziah on 1,5, the rest in their old tiles), and it lost Keziah and Pell on turn 10 with one Recall. The Sim also said that order was worse (41 percent against 62).
Tension: 6/10   Choice: 7/10   Surprise: 6/10
Best turn: player phase 6. The foreman was on 28 on his hill. Dunstan hit him from 13,7 for 8 and ate both gauntlet hits (17 to 5). Teodor hit from 12,6 for 9 and ate both (21 to 5). Ottilie shot him from 11,6 for 5 and took no counter, because he still had the gauntlets in hand. Pell cast from 12,7 for 12 on 6 HP, 94 percent. Four strikes, 28 to 0, and two of my people were left on 5.

The opening screen now says a unit standing on 7,0 stops the rider, and the plug stopped being a secret: the captain walked there by turn 3, and both north lines printed `is blocked: its tile is held`. With north2 on phase 5, one plug buys two waves that both land while the bridge is being fought. That is a real price, because the captain is out of the fight from turn 2 to turn 6. Keziah is the point of the issue, and the forecast makes it: against the Def 8 shieldbearer the axe reads 8 x2 and the fists 3 x4. I took the axe. She left it on 8, and Pell finished it. That is a good rule, and nothing on screen says it except the forecast. It was not tense: with the plug known and the order known, the map is a clean six turns. A cold player will not have either, so Harrow Weir still needs a cold play from Lotus or a fresh Chat body.

## 2026-09-26 — Ironwake Keep, both walls rebuilt (issue 82, experiment) — Code

Seed: 82   Result: win, survive, after turn 8 of 8. Ottilie and Wren fell. No Recall spent.
Not cold: I drew the keep and the menu an hour before. The scores rate the spike, not a tuned map.
Tension: 7/10   Choice: 6/10   Surprise: 7/10
Best turn: player phase 5. Teodor was on 6 at the south breach with an archer, a soldier and a hexer queued behind it. I pulled him back to dress, and `threat captain from 11,7` read three lines, but the total was 18 against 22: with 10,8 rebuilt, only 10,7 and 9,7 can reach that tile, so one enemy per strike tile means two. The captain held the breach himself and came out on 14. That is the edit working the way 13.5 promised. The 800 I spent turned a gap into a sum I could read.
Notes: The same numbers came back as a warning. On enemy phase 5 the hexer walked to 9,5 and cast over the wall into Ottilie on 11,5 for exactly her 10. 9,5 is a ditch tile on the menu, and I had not bought it. The keep taught me what the ditch is for by killing someone, which is the best argument the experiment could make for it. Wren's fall was my mistake: Pell had already killed the brigand, and I stepped Wren into 11,4 anyway, into an archer at range 2 and a soldier on the breach. What wasn't tense: turns 1 and 2. The first wave stops short of the breaches and waits, so the opening is a free setup. The Sim disagrees with me about the walls. The heuristic wins 85 percent on the bare keep and 62 with both walls, because it sorties through the breaches rather than holding them, and a narrower breach traps its sortie. Gate 1 cannot judge a defensive edit, and that is itself a result: if the keep is kept, its acceptance is play, not the gate.

## 2026-09-26 — Ironwake Keep, ditch and both forts (issue 82, experiment) — Chat

Seed: 91   Result: win, survive, after turn 8 of 8. Nobody fell. One Recall spent.

Tension: 6/10   Choice: 6/10   Surprise: 5/10
Best turn: player phase 5. Dunstan had held the north breach at 10,3 for two phases and was on 1 HP with brigand-3 (11 HP) on 9,3. Chat pulled him to 12,2, stepped Wren from 10,4 into 10,3, and moved Pell into the 10,4 she had left, so Pell killed the brigand at range 2 with no counter and the breach was never open for a step. In the south, Ottilie took the hexer from 16 to 0 from 10,8 with both hits, eating the 10 between them. The captain and Teodor then walked out and killed the archer. Three kills that phase, and in the whole battle no enemy got past the breach line.
Notes: The fort at 11,4 was Pell's perch all game, with two kills from it at range 2 and no counter. But 11,4 is two tiles from 9,4 whether it is a fort or plain, so what was bought was 3 HP of heal and some avoid, never felt. The ditch was never seen: the hexer came in from 0,8 along the south, stopped on 8,8, and died there. A defensive edit that works is invisible, because what it prevented never happens. Recall to turn 6, killed soldier-3 from inside, and held. The keep punishing a sortie is the keep working. ## 2026-09-26 — Carry the fallen, two plays (DESIGN 13.8, experiment) — Code

Seed: 73 on Brackwater Cut with `keepsakes: on` (`docs/samples/brackwater_cut_keepsakes.map`), and 6 on the raid with `keepsakes: on` (`docs/samples/ironwake_raid_keepsakes.map`). Result: Brackwater, escape on turn 7 with four out and Dunstan fallen, his lance left at 12,3. The raid, a rout on turn 4 with Teodor fallen and his lance recovered by Dunstan.
Not cold: I wrote the rule an hour before. Brackwater replays Chat's seed 73 exit script through enemy phase 6. On the raid, the heuristic's own trace runs to the end of enemy phase 2 (the only way I had to get a death without throwing one), and I played by hand from turn 3.
Tension: 5/10   Choice: 4/10   Surprise: 5/10 (the keepsake, not the maps)
Best turn: the raid, player phase 3. Teodor's lance was on 8,7, two tiles from the hexer. The captain killed the archer, Wren's double took the hexer to 5, and Pell finished it from 7,6 at 99. Only then did Dunstan walk onto the tile and pick up the lance, and `show dunstan` read `2: Iron Lance (Teodor's) x37`. That line was the best thing the spike made. It is also the whole problem: by the time the grave was safe, picking the lance up cost nothing.
Notes: The question was whether anyone would spend a turn walking back into the place a recruit fell. In two plays the rule never asked it. On Brackwater, Dunstan fell on 12,3, the plug, and brigand-2 was standing on his lance before my turn 7 started. Recovering it meant killing a 23 HP brigand and then leaving the recoverer behind, so nobody went. On the raid, the grave was in the middle of the fight until the fight was over. After that it was free, and Dunstan already carries an iron lance, so the second one is a name and not a weapon. The rule has two states, unreachable and free, and neither one is a decision. One fact from the Sim traces: all 20 heuristic raids on seeds 1 to 20 left at least one keepsake, and eleven had two keepsakes on one tile or a spell (Pell's cinder) left as one. The rule handles both, but two weapons stacked on 7,5 read like a junk shop, not a grave. My lean is not to kill it yet: 13.8 is the only thing in the game that remembers a dead recruit after the battle, and the name line earned its place. The name alone is decoration, though. What I would try next, and will not build without the Table: an enemy that ends its phase on a keepsake takes it, and carries it until it dies (`brigand-2 carries Teodor's lance`). That enemy becomes the target, and where it falls the keepsake drops again. That turns Brackwater's unreachable case into the tense one.
Transcript: docs/transcripts/2026-09-26-brackwater_cut_keepsakes-73.txt and docs/transcripts/2026-09-26-ironwake_raid_keepsakes-6.txt, each with its script beside it. A test replays the raid under `--strict`.

## 2026-09-26 — The carrier arm on Brackwater and the keep (13.8, issue 295) — Code

Seeds: Brackwater Cut with `keepsakes: on`, seed 88; the keep with the header, seed 5. Brackwater: escape on turn 7 with four out and Rook fallen on turn 1. The keep: survived after turn 8, with Pell fallen on enemy phase 6 and Dunstan on enemy phase 8. No Recall spent in either.
Not cold: I wrote the rule. I played both by hand from turn 1, asking `threat` and `forecast` before each commit.
Tension: 6/10   Choice: 5/10   Surprise: 4/10 (the maps carried the tension; the carrier never appeared)
Best turn: the keep, player phase 7. Pell's Cinder lay at 9,5, outside the wall, with brigand-4 beside it and a hexer two turns off, which could wield it. The only unit that could reach it and live was the captain, and `threat captain from 9,5` read 23 against 22. I left it. That was a real decision about a keepsake, the first one in three plays, and it was priced by the threat line, not by the rule. Brackwater had nothing like it. Rook flew into the fort archer's reach on turn 1 and died at 12,2, and on turn 2 Dunstan took the lance on his way through the gap for 3 HP from the same archer. That was the free state again.
Notes: The carrier never happened. In both plays, no enemy ended a move on a grave. On the keep, brigand-4 struck Pell from 8,5 and then walked past 9,5 toward the captain, and the grave sat at the edge of the enemy stream for two phases. The planner scores attack tiles and approach tiles, and a keepsake is on neither list, so an enemy picks one up only when a grave happens to be its best tile. Chat's point from the raid cuts the other way here: people die on contested tiles, but on a defence the enemy is pushing past those tiles, not standing on them. The end-of-battle lines are the best new thing: `Cinder (Pell's) was left at 9,5` and `Iron Lance (Dunstan's) was left at 11,8` close the keep, and they read as a cost. The kill criterion has not been met or missed, because no carrier existed to ignore. So the next question is whether the planner should want the stack. One lean I have not built: an enemy that can wield a keepsake scores its tile as an attack option. Seed 73's line with this build does show the arm working: brigand-2 walks onto Dunstan's lance at 12,3 in the same enemy phase he falls there, on its way through the gap, and the escape ends with `Iron Lance (Dunstan's) went with brigand-2`.
Transcripts: docs/transcripts/2026-09-26-brackwater_cut_keepsakes-88.txt and docs/transcripts/2026-09-26-ironwake_keep_keepsakes-5.txt, each with its script beside it. Both play under `--strict`, and a test replays the keep's.

## 2026-09-26 — Brackwater Cut at dusk (DESIGN 13.7, experiment) — Code

Seed: 7 on `docs/samples/brackwater_cut_dusk.map` (the shipped map plus `dusk: 7`, so sight runs 7, 6, 5, 4, 3, 2, 1, 1). Result: escape on turn 5 with Rook, Wren and the captain out; Pell fell on enemy phase 1 and Dunstan on enemy phase 4. One Recall spent, on turn 1, after Rook walked into the fort archer's 19.
Not cold: I wrote the rule. By hand from turn 1, asking `threat` and `forecast` before committing.
Tension: 6/10   Choice: 5/10   Surprise: 6/10
Best turn: player phase 4. Dunstan stood in the gap at 11,3 on 8 HP. `threat dunstan` priced four enemies at 27 against his 8, then said `and whatever is in the dark (?), unpriced`, with two question marks at 4,3 and 5,3 behind them. Moving him east still read 21. So he held and waited. The captain went up the north edge to 18,0, five tiles from the bank's sleeping `?`s, and Dunstan died in the gap on the enemy phase, with the hexer's 99 last. On turn 5 the board showed ten `?`s and three letters in the top-right corner, and I walked out. That board is the best picture the spike made.
Notes: The dark worked as a picture and as a turn-2 surprise. On turn 1, archer-1 was a `?` at 0,5, and on enemy phase 3 it walked out of the dark into the fight and put 3 into Dunstan before I knew which `?` it was. The bank at 17,5 to 17,7 went dark on turn 2. I planned the whole north-edge route around the wake radius, measured against three question marks. But the `?` shows where, and on this map, where was all I needed. The wake rule counts tiles, not names. So the dark took away who, and on an Escape map who matters least, because you are leaving it. The strike rule never fired. Nobody tried a shot at range 2 on a night of sight 1, because by then I was out. The console hides what it should: moves in the dark print as `enemy: something moves in the dark` (36 lines in the transcript, the recalled enemy phase included), and `threat` never prices a `?`. The protocol still shows everything, since the spike only hides things in the console. My lean: keep it for one more arm, and judge it on a map where you have to walk into the dark, not away from it. That means a Seize or a Defeat Boss, where the objective sits beyond sight by turn 4. Sallow Grange at `dusk: 9` is the obvious candidate: the Reeve's hall goes dark just as you have to open it. A second lever, if that arm plays soft: hide the `?` too beyond some hearing radius, so the dark takes where as well as who.
Transcript: docs/transcripts/2026-09-26-brackwater_cut_dusk-7.txt, with its script beside it. It plays under `--strict`, and a test replayed it until the third arm (#308) changed the rules it was played under.

## 2026-09-26 — Brackwater Cut at dusk 5, symmetric knowledge (DESIGN 13.7, second arm, issue 302) — Code

Seed: 5 on `docs/samples/brackwater_cut_dusk5.map` (dusk 5). Result: escape on turn 7 of 8, all five out, nobody fell, no Recall. I restarted once from turn 1 before this line: in the first attempt Rook missed the fort archer and Pell died to it on enemy phase 1. That attempt is not in the transcript.
Not cold: I wrote the rule. By hand.
Tension: 6/10   Choice: 6/10   Surprise: 5/10
Best turn: player phase 5, sight 1. Dunstan had held the holding tile at 12,3 for two enemy phases, killed the brigand on a counter, and was down to 5 HP with rider-1 beside him in the gap. `threat dunstan from 13,1` priced the rider at 6 against 5. `threat dunstan from 14,1` read `rider-1: cannot see you (dark)`, because 14,1 is five tiles from the gap, one past hearing. One tile was the difference between his death and his escape. He walked to 14,1, and the chase stood still.
Notes: On enemy phase 1 only the brigand and rider-1 moved. They knew where we were through the fort archer's sight, and the rest of the chase sat on the west edge. The bank's wake radius gives the map a staging area, 18,0, 19,0, 18,1, 19,1 and 19,2: each is in reach of 19,3 and more than 4 from every bank guard. The whole party staged there and left in order, the captain last. That is daylight geometry, though, not the dark. **The soft part:** once Dunstan was out of hearing, nothing in the dark knew anything, and the last three turns were a walk. On an Escape map, symmetric knowledge makes the dark free cover once you are past the wall. That is my forty-sixth-round worry, arriving where the map lets you leave. The hearing rule kept the fight at the gap honest, and it did not make the walk to the exit cost anything. The rule changed a move (14,1, not 13,1) and a plan (the staging tiles against hearing), so by the kill criterion it is not dead from this chair.

## 2026-09-26 — Saltmarsh Ford with the Ford Chief, `arsenal: on` (DESIGN 13.11, experiment) — Code

Seed: 31 on `docs/samples/saltmarsh_ford_chief.map` (the shipped map, the bandit leader replaced by the Ford Chief: the same stats with Steel Axe, Toll Axe and Hatchet, and `arsenal: on`). Result: rout on turn 9 of 18. Nobody fell. Two Recalls: one on turn 2 after Wren's double missed the 2 HP brigand and left her in two enemies' reach, one on turn 8 after Teodor died to the Hatchet.
Not cold: I wrote the header. By hand, asking `threat` and `forecast` before each commit.
Tension: 7/10   Choice: 7/10   Surprise: 6/10
Best turn: player phase 8, the second time. The chief held the Toll Axe from my own bait on turn 7, and with it every cadet's double was gone: `counter with Toll Axe`, and the captain's 7 x2 read 7. The first time through I had put Teodor beside the fort at 7 HP, and the Hatchet killed him at 91 percent, which `threat` had already told me. After the Recall, `threat teodor from 10,1` at 18 HP read `with Steel Axe (slot 2): dmg 16 hit 74%`. So Teodor stood there and did not attack. The chief took the heavy axe, Teodor came out on 2 and put 8 into it on the counter, and on turn 9 the Steel Axe was still in its hand: Ottilie 4 unanswered at range 2, Wren 6 x2 into a 17 counter she could survive, and the captain's first strike finished it at 2 HP, so it never swung back.
Notes: **The weapon it last swung is the one it counters with**, and that rule was already in the game (section 5's Items rule, issue 99). Before this header, nothing on the screen said it. On shipped Saltmarsh the bandit leader already switches between Steel Axe and Toll Axe against the same captain in the heuristic's traces, so this was a hidden choice the whole time. With both weapons named, the boss fight became a question about what to feed it. Heavy is the dangerous counter, but the cadets double it. Light is the safer counter, but nobody doubles it. The Toll Axe is the one that answers Ottilie at range 2. I got it wrong first (the range-2 bait cost me the doubles I needed) and right second. What stays soft: the Hatchet and the Toll Axe are nearly the same answer against this cast, because both are at or under the chief's Str, so the real split is heavy against everything else. And the trick only works on a boss that holds, because an Aggressive enemy swings every phase and resets its own counter.
Transcript: docs/transcripts/2026-09-26-saltmarsh_ford_chief-31.txt, with its script beside it. It plays under `--strict`, and a test replays it.

Pell at 10,2 then read `dmg 10 hit 91%` against the 10 left, and it fell. If Rook had missed, Pell would have eaten an 8 at 86 and the archer would have healed 3 in its fort. It was the only real gamble of the night.
Notes: **The gap is a cork.** I left Wren on 11,3 to guard the rear, and the whole chase stood still: nine `something in the dark acts` lines on each of enemy phases 1 to 4, and on turn 5 the `unseen at` line was the same as it had been on turn 1. Drift paths around the party, and 11,3 is the only land way east, so no chase unit had a path to an exit and each one Waited. I only understood why by reading `EnemyAi.Drift`, so a cold player would find it by accident, the way I did, and then keep doing it. Once Wren left, the chase came along row 3 and was still west of the wall at the end. The only enemy contact was the bank, and that was my mistake: I stopped Wren at 15,3, exactly 4 from 17,5, and the shieldbearer woke and hit her for 8. On enemy phase 6 the shieldbearer and the soldier both swung at her at 18,2 (60 and 63) and both missed. Turn 7 was the door leak: Wren and the captain walked through 19,3 past both. The tense part was real, about ten seconds of it. The rest was a walk. Filed #326, with the lean that drift paths as if the party were not there, so the chase comes to the cork and meets it at sight 1.
Transcript: docs/transcripts/2026-09-26-brackwater_cut-41.txt (script beside it). It plays under `--strict`, and a test replays it.

**Brawler on the Gate (the shipped sample).** Result: seize on player phase 10 of 10. Pell fell. Two Recalls (Chat's on turn 2, mine on turn 9).
Tension: 8/10   Choice: 7/10   Surprise: 8/10

That left the captain on 10 beside a brawler that deals 6 x2, so I recalled to keep him out of reach. Second time, Ansgar spotted from 16,5 and Cantoed away to 16,4, Pell and Ottilie shot from the ring, and the captain waited at 14,5, three from the Gate. On the enemy phase the brawler stepped off to 15,6 and killed Pell with 9 x2. The Gate was empty, and the captain walked onto it on turn 10. I checked the first line as well: the brawler steps off for Pell there too, and passes over the captain on 10.
Notes: **The rule makes the throne cost a body, not a fight.** From enemy phase 3 to 8 the brawler waited on 16,6, because it could strike nobody. Its kill score then pulled it off the one tile it was there to hold. The casters on the ring were the bait, as Chat guessed, and I paid for the throne with Pell. Chat's slog test says this is not a slog: about 12.7 net a phase against 23 hp at level 1, driven by Pell's free 10 at 73. The replay agrees, since one volley took 13. The step-off is what I will remember. My Recall on turn 9 was wasted, because the brawler goes for Pell either way.

## 2026-09-26 — Brackwater Cut at dusk 5, drift through the party (issue 326) — Code

Seed: 53 on `content/maps/brackwater_cut.map`. Result: escape on player phase 7 of 8. Wren, Rook and the captain out; Dunstan and Pell fell. One Recall (turn 2).
Not cold: I built the change. By hand.
Tension: 8/10   Choice: 7/10   Surprise: 8/10
Best turn: player phase 6. On enemy phase 5 a rider drifted up row 3 and stopped on 19,3, the exit I had staged everyone beside. It was a `b` on the board only because Pell stood next to it. I pulled Rook and Wren back north, stepped Pell to 19,1 and the captain to 19,2; the captain hit it for 10 and made it visible, and Pell's Cinder did 12 at 99 from range 2 with no counter. The rider fell, and the noise woke the bank.
Notes: **The cork is a fight now.** Dunstan sat on 11,3 and the chase came to him: rider-1 on enemy phase 2 (6 in, 10 back), then the brigand for 9 on phase 3, and he fell on the gap. That bought one turn, which was the turn the party needed to get past the watchtower and up to row 1. On turn 1 I sent Pell at the fort archer after Rook missed; the archer's counter left Pell on 8 and the enemy phase killed him, so I recalled into turn 1 before Pell's strike and let him wait instead. The archer then shot Dunstan for 3 rather than Pell. The chase in the dark is still readable: a line of `?` along row 3 coming through the gap, one tile a turn closer. Losses: Rook died on enemy phase 4 to an archer's 19 in an earlier draft of this script, when a move I miscounted left him west of the wall; the kept script moves him to 15,1. Pell died on enemy phase 6 at 19,1 to a brigand coming round from 18,3, after the shot that won the exit. I lost a second unit because I spent the turn clearing the exit, and I would do it again. The bank is still the map's clock at the door: every exit tile is within 4 of 17,5, so the first unit out wakes it.

## 2026-09-26 — Old Mill Road with grudges (DESIGN 13.4, experiment/grudges) — Code

Seed: 65 on `docs/samples/old_mill_road_grudges.map` (Old Mill Road with `grudges: on`). Result: rout on player phase 8 of 12. Nobody fell. One Recall (turn 7).
Not cold: I built the rule. By hand.
Tension: 7/10   Choice: 8/10   Surprise: 7/10
Best turn: player phase 7, the second time through. The first time, Wren stood on 5,1 at 11 hp, sworn on by both the archer and the mill bandit. She missed the bandit, and its swing on the enemy phase killed her. After the Recall I did the opposite. The captain stepped off the fort to 7,1, hit the bandit to 5 and took 9 back, down to 4 hp on plain beside it. Wren took the fort. `threat captain` read `no enemy can strike it next phase`, and it was true: both enemies were sworn on Wren, and she was in reach, so neither turned to the 4 hp captain. The bandit struck Wren on the fort for 8, and her counter killed it. The grudge was the captain's only armor, and I paid for it with Wren's hp.
Notes: **Who lands the last hit is now a decision.** On turn 2 I let Wren finish the brigand. The road archer swore on her, and its bow went from the 7 hp captain to a full-hp Wren. On turn 6 the captain softened the soldier to 2 and Wren finished it, which put both surviving mill guards on her. That was the same trade, but this time it nearly cost her. The rule gives the kill order a price, and the row (`sworn: wren`) plus `threat` show the price before you commit, so it never felt like a trick. **It is a lure, and I used it as one every time.** Each grudge I chose was meant to pull enemies off the captain, which is the kill criterion's worry. It stayed a decision only because Wren could not afford every grudge: on turn 7 the lure nearly killed the lure. On a map where the player has a real tank to hand the kills to, I expect it to be free. The Sim barely moved (gate 1 65 of 200 with and without the header; Wren's attacks 272 against 273), because the heuristic takes kills by score and never thinks about who will be hunted. I stopped caring once the mill was down. The archer left over was one sworn body, and with nobody to protect there was no choice left.
Transcript: docs/transcripts/2026-09-26-old_mill_road_grudges-65.txt (script beside it).

## 2026-09-26 — Old Mill Road with grudges, the sworn crit (issue 331, 0066's replay) — Code

Seed: 65 on `docs/samples/old_mill_road_grudges.map`, my own script from the first play, replayed under the second pass. Result: rout on player phase 8 of 12, as before. Nobody fell. One Recall (turn 7).
Not cold: I built both passes. By hand, plus one probe line off the script.
Tension: 7/10   Choice: 7/10   Surprise: 6/10
Best turn: player phase 7, line B again. The captain is on 4 hp at 7,1 beside the 5 hp bandit, and Wren is on the fort at 11. `threat wren` now reads the archer at `dmg 4 hit 43% crit 20%` and the bandit at `dmg 8 hit 63% crit 23%`. Each crit alone kills her (12 and 24 against 11). The fort was no longer safe: her death chance went from about 28 percent to about 39. And about 9 percent of the time the archer crits her first, the bandit loses its sworn target, and it turns to the 4 hp captain. Before this change, the captain's risk on that line was zero. I probed the one other move I had, Wren finishing the bandit from the fort. The keyed roll makes that swing a miss, the same miss that forced the Recall, and she dies on the enemy phase. So I kept the fort line and took the bet with the number in view. No crit landed. The phase played out as it did before.
Notes: **The crit did not change a decision in my replay.** It changed the price of the one I made. I saw the number and paid it knowingly, but that is not a changed decision, and by the kill criterion my replay counts toward the kill. The arm now rests on Chat's seed 23. Chat's script still replays under `--strict`. Its turn 8 bandit strike on Wren is now `crit 23%` (it was 3), against 13 hp and a 24 crit, and the new log line names the lure outright: `grudge: mill_bandit-1 strikes sworn wren (score 0.6); best alternative captain (score 97.1)`. The log is the best thing in this pass. On my own seed 65 turn 7 line A, it reads `score 98.7; best alternative captain (score 101.5)`. The grudge turned the bandit off a 6 hp captain it scored as a kill, by three points of score, and it has done that every time without anyone seeing it. What I would watch for in Chat's replay: whether the 23 percent makes Chat hand the turn 8 kill to the captain instead of Wren. That is the kill reassignment the criterion names.

## 2026-09-26 — Brackwater Cut at dusk with shove (DESIGN 13.12, experiment/shove) — Code

Seed: 29 on `docs/samples/brackwater_cut_shove.map`, the shipped map with `shove: on`. Result: lost on enemy phase 7. Rook exited on turn 4. Dunstan, Pell and Wren fell, then a 2 hp rider found the 5 hp captain one tile from the exit lane. One Recall, on turn 4.
Not cold: I built the rule earlier in the same run. By hand, turn by turn.
Tension: 6/10   Choice: 6/10   Surprise: 5/10
Best turn: turn 6, and shove had no part in it. The captain was on 5 hp in the pocket at 12,2. The wall, the water, Wren and Dunstan closed it on four sides, so at sight 1 no enemy could see it and `threat` read `no enemy can strike it`. That pocket is the best tile on Brackwater and I had never noticed it. With Wren dead the pocket had one wall missing. So I ran the captain south to 12,6, out of hearing, and left Dunstan in the gap. He took the brawler to 1 hp and died to the brigand behind it.
Notes: **I never shoved.** I looked for one every turn, and each time it was illegal or worthless:
- Turn 3: Dunstan in the gap and the rider at 10,3. Pushing it to 9,3 buys nothing, because it walks back with its whole move and swings anyway, so I struck.
- Turn 5, and again later: the enemy in the gap had the next pursuer behind it on 10,3, so the tile beyond was never open. A one-tile corridor is a conga line, and shove needs the tile behind the target.
- Turn 4: the bank's shieldbearer, the one unit I badly wanted moved, is heft 15 against Dunstan's 14.
- Turn 4, Wren on 5 hp: the captain pushing her one tile east would have added a tile to her move, but she was still one short of the exit.

The structural problem is that a push happens on the player phase, and the enemy phase then undoes it with a full move. Shove as built is worth something only against a unit that will not move (a Hold archer, a sleeping Guard, the seated Reeve), or as +1 tile of tempo for an ally. Brackwater has no reachable Hold unit (the fort archer is ringed by water and wall). So on this map it answers nothing about what the corridor should give Dunstan to hold. If Chat's play also finds no shove, I lean toward a second arm where the push costs the pushed unit its next move (staggered), rather than a kill. But that is a new rule, and it goes to the Table first.
Transcript: docs/transcripts/2026-09-26-brackwater_cut_shove-29.txt (script: docs/transcripts/2026-09-26-brackwater_cut_shove-29.script).

## 2026-09-26 — Sallow Grange with shove (DESIGN 13.12, experiment/shove) — Chat

Map: Sallow Grange with the `shove: on` header added in Chat's sandbox. Seed 41, cold. Won the seize on turn 8 with one Recall; Teodor, Wren and Ottilie lost.

Notes: An archer crit on turn 2 took Teodor from full, and the Recall on turn 3 kept Pell alive. The game said `pell cannot shove captain: heft 3 is under its 13`. The captain parked beside the throne, Ottilie died on the enemy phase, and the seize came a turn late. The best turn not taken was that push; it is the most interesting decision the rule offered in either partner's play. Led to #344 (heft only against enemies).

## 2026-09-27 — Sallow Grange with shove, allies without heft (DESIGN 13.12, issue 344) — Code

Seed: 83 on `docs/samples/sallow_grange_shove.map`. Result: won the seize on turn 6, nobody lost, one Recall (turn 3).
Not cold: I built #344 earlier in the same run. By hand, turn by turn.
Tension: 7/10   Choice: 8/10   Surprise: 7/10
Best turn: turn 5, a rescue relay. Ottilie killed the hexer from 12,6 and was left on 6 hp beside the woken Reeve. Ansgar had a strike on the Reeve from 14,6 and took a shove instead: he stepped onto 13,6, the enemy's side of her, and pushed her west to 11,6. `threat` then showed the Toll Spear still reaching her from 12,5 at range 2. So the captain walked round to 12,6 and pushed her again, to 10,6, and Teodor closed the gate at 11,5. `threat ottilie` read `no enemy can strike it`. The Reeve spent his phase on Ansgar instead (10 hp).
Notes: Four pushes in the game, all on allies, all after first contact, two relays of two.
- Turn 5, on Ottilie: the rescue above. The rescue geometry from the fifty-ninth round held up: pulling a done ally back costs the pusher a tile on the enemy's side, and it cost Ansgar 10 hp.
- Turn 6, on the captain: the Reeve stayed on 15,6 and struck from range 2, so the throne's row was shut, and the captain on 12,6 was six tiles from 16,6 by any path. Ottilie stepped to 11,6 and pushed him to 13,6. Teodor stepped to 12,6 and pushed him to 14,6. Both of them could have struck the Reeve instead. The captain, still unmoved, walked 14,5, 15,5, 16,5, 16,6.
This is the turn Chat was refused on seed 41, and it plays as well as Chat said it would. Three recruits throwing the captain past a boss is a memorable turn. It is also one turn faster than the fastest daylight seize on file (turn 7, seed 31). Under the sixtieth round's trigger (a relay before first contact, or a relay-assisted seize on turn 5 or earlier), it does not cap. It is one turn from the line, so the next play should watch it.
Enemy pushes: none. Both heavy targets are still out of reach (the Reeve 14, the shieldbearer 15). The hexer was a kill, not a push.
The turn 3 Recall was not about shove: the captain and Ottilie both missed the brawler, which left the captain on 10 hp beside it (12 if both gauntlet strikes landed). No push could get him clear, because every tile beyond him was held.

## 2026-09-27 — The Tollgate, rider at 13,4, Code's re-rate (issue 351, sixty-third round) — Code

Seed: 199 on `content/maps/the_tollgate.map` at 6d81dcf. Result: won the seize on turn 8 of 10 with two Recalls spent; Pell lost. The boss was never touched.
Not cold: I had read Chat's seed 113 entry and knew the rider came from 13,4 on enemy phase 4, so I kept the captain out of its reach on turn 4. A fresh seed, by hand, with a different staging (Teodor and Pell east of the river, the captain behind them, Wren alone on the west).
Tension: 7/10   Choice: 7/10   Surprise: 6/10
Best turn: turn 7. The warden was dead, the archer and the boss were whole, Pell was on 7 hp, and the throne was two tiles past the archer. The captain hit the archer from 6,2 (11 at 87) and Pell finished it from the door (13 at 99). That opened 6,1 for a turn-8 seize but left Pell on 6,3 with 7 hp and the boss's Toll Axe 14 at 65 on her. The slow line was the captain alone on the archer and a seize on turn 9. I took the fast one and the boss took Pell. It was the right trade and it hurt.
Notes: This was the fast clear the sixty-second round asked about. The brigand fell on turn 3 (Teodor 11 at 47, then Pell 13 at 87), the woods archer on turn 4 (Wren missed twice at 54, Pell and Teodor's 41 finished it). The rider then arrived at 13,4, rode row 4 and stopped on 8,5, adjacent to nobody, because every one of my units was in forest or behind it. On turn 5, Pell (12 at 99, no counter at range 2) and Wren (9 at 85) killed it before it swung. So on a fast clear with the arrival respected, the rider costs a turn and two actions and never strikes. That is Code's 181 finding again from the other end: slow or fast, the rider hurts only a party that stands in row 4's reach on enemy phase 4, which Chat's seed 113 party did and mine did not. The tension came from the door, not the rider. Turn 6 was the best-priced turn of the map: Teodor's warden attack was a known miss, and a Recall to try it in the other order found that order does not change the roll (the key is the attack, not its place in the turn). I spent the second Recall to take Teodor off the door and leave Pell on 6,4 at 7 hp against the warden's 37; it missed and died on her counter. The console had told me the rolls do not change, and I paid a charge to learn that order is not a way around it. Surprise is 6: nothing on the board did what I did not expect, and the boss never had to die, because a seize walks past him on 6,1. The Tollgate is not `tuned` on this entry.

## 2026-09-27 — The Tollgate, the rider on the door (issue 370) — Code

Seed: 211 on `content/maps/the_tollgate.map` with `riders enter 6,4 6,3`. Result: won the seize on turn 9 of 10 with one Recall spent; Pell lost. The boss was never touched.
Not cold: I built #370 in the same run and wrote the trigger, so I knew exactly where and when the rider would come. The opening is my seed 199 staging.
Tension: 8/10   Choice: 7/10   Surprise: 7/10
Best turn: turn 5. The woods had fallen on turn 4, a fast clear, so under the clock the rider would already have been scenery. Teodor stepped onto 6,3 to price the warden, and the rider arrived at 13,4 on that move, mid-turn, with three of my units still to act. Teodor's door then read 23 against 17 if all three keep strikes landed. I had to answer the rider with the actions I had left. I put the captain on the 7,5 forest as bait (7 at 26, with an 87 percent counter for 10). The rider took the bait from 8,5, the captain's counter put it on 11, and Pell's crit killed it on turn 6. On the same phase the boss's axe missed Teodor, and Teodor's counter left the warden on 1.
Notes: The change did what the sixty-fifth round asked. On a fast clear the old rider arrived into an empty flank. This one arrived on the door, and I could not step around it, only pay for it. Here I paid with the captain's hp and the turn-6 tempo: the warden sat on 1 hp for a full turn, because the only unit that could reach 6,3 was the captain, and the boss's Steel Axe read 16 against his 15 there. On turn 8 Wren's 85 on the archer was a known miss. I spent a Recall to take her, on 2 hp, off 6,2, and the boss took Pell on 6,3 instead, as on seed 199. Pell is now the door's usual price, which is worth watching. Surprise is 7 from a warm chair: nothing surprised me about where the rider came from, but its arrival mid-turn turned an ordinary door opener into a split turn. `threat` before the step did not list the rider, which is right for an unannounced event (0045), but a cold player has no way to learn that the step onto 6,3 is the trigger.

## 2026-09-27 — The Tollgate, the rider on the door, Chat's re-rate (issue 370, sixty-eighth round) — Chat

Seed: 227 on `content/maps/the_tollgate.map` with `riders enter 6,4 6,3`. Result: won the seize on turn 8 of 10 with no losses and no Recalls spent.
Not cold: I wrote the trigger, and I knew the tile and the arrival reach.
Tension: 8/10   Choice: 7/10   Surprise: 7/10
Best turn: turn 6. The rider had parked on 7,4, and with Wren on 6,4 and Teodor on 6,3 it corked the door. It was not a second front. It was a body in the gap. Pell went to 5,4 and killed it at 99 percent. Then I spent the whole rest of the turn untangling: Wren out to the 5,5 forest and a Dressing, Teodor back through 6,4 to 7,4 and a Dressing. I pressed nothing on a warden at 11. It felt like losing a turn. It was actually buying one.
Notes: I opened as I did on 113. Pell's Cinder killed the brigand on turn 4, then three misses in a row left the woods archer on 7, and Pell finished it on turn 5. On turn 5 Teodor stepped onto 6,3 and hit the warden from 21 to 11, and the rider arrived on that step with Wren and the captain still to act. `threat` showed that from 6,3 alone the rider has no target on arrival, because 7,4 touches 6,4, 7,5 and 8,4, not 6,3. I baited it anyway: Wren onto the 6,4 forest and the captain onto 7,5. Both low rolls landed, and Wren went from 20 to 5. At the end of turn 5 Teodor was at roughly 40 percent to die, and I took that gamble rather than a Recall. On turn 7 Pell took the warden from 6,4, and on turn 8 Teodor killed the archer and the captain walked 6,2, 6,1, 7,1. Choice is 7: who stands where when the rider comes, and then whether to press or untangle on turn 6. Surprise is 7: I knew the trigger, but I did not foresee the rider jamming the door with its own body. Wren paid the toll, taking the boss's 12 on turn 7 for my careless parking on 7,4. The keep collected a turn, not a body.

## 2026-09-27 — Brackwater Cut at dusk, Chat's re-rate (seventieth round) — Chat

Dunstan held the gap, stepping back to 12,3 on turn 4, where only 11,3 can melee him and only 10,3 can shoot him; he took brigand-1's 9 twice and gave back 11 twice. Turn 5, he killed it at 77, and Rook, Pell, Wren and the captain went out through 19,3. The first step onto 19,3 woke the bank, and the bank never got a phase. Neither rider ever swung. Tension is 5: nobody but Dunstan was ever in reach, and he was never under half before turn 4. Choice is 6: the turn 2 ordering and the 12,3 step-back were the only real decisions. Surprise is 4: nothing did anything I had not counted. The finding: the bank can be dodged by reading, the Tollgate's fault. The staging pocket is a puzzle once; a warm player solves it on turn 1 and the bank is scenery. Lean filed as #377.

## 2026-09-27 — Brackwater Cut at dusk, exit without a Move (issue 377) — Code

Seed: 241 on `content/maps/brackwater_cut.map` with issue 377's rule. Result: won by escape on turn 6 of 8, no Recalls; Wren, Pell and the captain out; Rook and Dunstan fell.
Not cold: turns 1 to 4 are Chat's seed 241 script move for move (the staging pocket on 19,0 to 19,2), and I built the rule. From turn 5 the play is mine.
Tension: 7/10   Choice: 6/10   Surprise: 6/10
Best turn: turn 5. The pocket was no longer an exit, only a waiting room. Dunstan killed brigand-1 on the gap (11 at 77), and then four units had to step onto 19,3 to 19,6 with the bank waking on the first step, and stand there through a phase. I put Rook, the flyer, furthest south on 19,6 so the walkers could have 19,3 to 19,5. The brawler came out of the dark at 17,7 and hit Rook twice for 10 (83 percent); Rook fell on the exit. The shieldbearer missed Wren on 19,3 and the soldier missed Pell on 19,5. The hexer killed Dunstan on the gap. On turn 6 Wren, Pell and the captain walked out.
Notes: The rule does what #377 asked. On Chat's play the bank never acted; here it took a body on its first phase, and the choice of who stands on which exit was the whole turn. I also tried the obvious alternative in a scratch replay (Rook on 19,3, the captain on 19,4, Pell on 19,5, Wren held back on 19,2): the brawler then took Pell (9 twice at 94), so the south exits belong to the brawler and whoever stands there is its price. Not in the transcript, and not counted as a Recall, because it was a separate run. What was not tense: turns 1 to 4 are unchanged, since the pocket is still safe to stage in; the tension all lands on turn 5. `threat` on an exit tile said `no enemy can strike it next phase` plus `whatever is in the dark (?), unpriced`, because at sight 1 the bank is unseen and asleep, so the console gives the new rule no warning beyond the legend. That is dusk working as built, but it means a cold player learns the brawler by losing a unit. Choice is 6 because the staging decides most of it. Surprise is 6: I knew the bank was there, but not which member would collect.

## 2026-09-27 — Harrow Weir, the Foreman wakes (issue 379) — Code

Seed: 379 on `content/maps/harrow_weir.map`. The Foreman is a guard boss in the weir group, carrying the Toll Axe alone. Result: won on turn 4 of 14, one Recall spent, nobody dead.
Not cold: I built the change and knew the wake radius. What I did not know was how he would come. My prediction on the Table was wrong.
Tension: 7/10   Choice: 6/10   Surprise: 8/10
Best turn: turn 4, after the Recall. The Foreman stood on 10,6 at 16 with the Toll Axe, and Pell was on 3 beside him on 9,6. Anything within 2 of him eats a 13-damage counter. Pell stepped off to 9,5 without acting. Dunstan stepped onto 9,6 and hit him for 9 at 73, taking 8 back. Then Pell finished him from 9,5 with the 99. If Dunstan had missed, Pell would have had to strike into a 67 percent counter on 3 hp.
Notes: On turn 3 I opened as the round predicted the reader would: Pell alone struck the shieldbearer from 9,6, and nobody stood on 10,6. The Foreman did not walk the south way. He walked through his own shieldbearer, since allies pass, stood on 10,6 and hit Pell for 13. On turn 4 I sent Ottilie (7 hp) to shoot him from 8,6 without reading the forecast, and the Toll Axe countered her at range 2 and killed her. I spent the Recall on that. So he took a Recall, and nearly a body in Pell. Before the Toll-Axe-only change, a scratch run on the same seed saw him kill Pell outright with the gauntlets (11 twice at 91). That run is not in the transcript and not counted. What was not tense: turns 1 and 2 are the old opening, and once he was dead on turn 4 the ford and the north waves were leftovers, not a fight. The pocket on 10,6 makes him killable in one turn by anyone who reads it: one melee tile and three ranged tiles. That is the open question for the re-rates.

Best turn: turn 5. The captain stepped onto 19,3 first, on purpose, to light the lamps before anyone else committed. The bank came up on the far edge with rows, and `threat` did the rest: 19,6 was 43 if all land against Rook's 17, with the brawler at 10x2 and 83; 19,3 was one shieldbearer lance at 7; 19,4 was 17 against Wren's 20. So Wren took 19,4, Pell and Rook stayed off the exits on 18,2 and 19,2, and nobody stood on 19,6. On the stand play Rook died there without a forecast; here the same brawler was a number I could refuse. Wren crit the soldier dead on its own swing.

On turn 6 it was a `?` again, and I moved Rook onto 19,4 beside it because I had stopped thinking about a unit I had seen a phase earlier. It took Rook 10x2 out of the dark. Second small moment: Wren's exit took the last eye off the shieldbearer on 18,4, so an attack I meant to make with Pell after her exit was refused (scratch replay). The order of exits is now a sight puzzle too. The finish was thin: Dunstan fell to the hexer holding the gap, Pell was the last body and could not stand an exit beside the brawler, so the captain left him. Not a re-rate; the map's Fun Gate re-rates stay with the Table.

## 2026-09-27 — Brackwater Cut at dusk, Code's re-rate on the built file (seventy-fifth round) — Code

Seed: 283 on `content/maps/brackwater_cut.map` at main 9c96e99 (#377 and #382 built; #385 touches only Defeat Boss). Result: won by escape on turn 6 of 8, one Recall; Wren, Rook and the captain out; Pell left behind; Dunstan fell.
Not cold: my seventh Brackwater play, I built both rules, and I had read Chat's seed 271 script. The route is my own from turn 1.
Tension: 7/10   Choice: 7/10   Surprise: 8/10
Best turn: turn 5, played twice. The lamps had shown the bank walking west off the exits on enemy phase 4, and at sight 1 they were `?` again: two on row 3 behind the gap and one at 14,6. The exits were empty, so the captain, Wren and Rook stepped onto 19,3 to 19,5. The only question was Pell. The first time, Pell killed rider-1 from 13,3, and the brawler came out of 14,6 and doubled him (9x2 at 94) while the gap's second wave killed Dunstan. I spent a Recall to move Pell to 12,2 for the same kill. 12,2 has one open neighbour, and the brawler could not reach it. Pell lived, Dunstan still fell, and the soldier missed Wren on the exit.
Notes: Turn 1 went clean. Rook hit the fort archer first (54, no counter), so Pell could finish it at 91 without taking the range-2 counter, and I would have kept Pell back on a miss. Dunstan corked 12,3, not 11,3, because 12,3 can be struck from only 11,3 and 10,3; Pell fired over him from 13,3 and later 12,2, and the gap became a kill zone. The surprise was mine to make and I did not see it coming: Pell's kill of brigand-1 on turn 4 was a combat 6 tiles from the shieldbearer, the noise radius, and the bank woke with its lamps lit while my pocket party was still two tiles short. I expected them to come for the pocket. Instead they went for the units the chase could see, the gap's defenders, because at dusk a side knows what any of its units sees, and nobody on their side could see the pocket. So the fight at the gap pulled the guards off the door. That is the lamps rule and the dusk knowledge rule working together in a way neither issue predicted. Turn 6: the captain, Wren and Rook left from the exits with the soldier on 13 beside Wren. Pell was two turns short, and I did not ask the captain to hold the edge for two more enemy phases to wait for him. Tension 7, not 8, because once the exits were empty the last two turns were sums I could do.

Under the keep's rule #385 passes: he collected 12 from Keziah, 10 from the captain, a Recall and about three turns. The front half is the Tollgate's door. The back half is not: after turn 7 nobody was at risk for three phases, because the veto refuses every strike from a worn boss, including the swing from the tile it has just chosen. The veto turned scenery into a runner, and a turn spent walking after someone who will not fight is not tense. Tension 6: turns 3 to 7 were a 7 or 8, turns 8 to 10 a 3.

## 2026-09-27 — Sallow Grange with the pincer (13.13, DECISIONS/0082) — Code

Seed: 97 on `docs/samples/sallow_grange_pincer.map` (the shipped Sallow Grange with `pincer: on`). Result: won on turn 8 of 10, one Recall spent, Ottilie dead.
Warm: I built the rule and have played Sallow before (seed 61, and the shove sample's 83). I took the long way: the field first, then the yard.
Tension: 7/10   Choice: 7/10   Surprise: 5/10
Best turn: turn 3. The field group had come to me: the soldier on 4,5, the brawler on 4,6 with Wren on 6 HP in front of it. Pell's Cinder killed the soldier. Then the question the pincer asks: Ansgar could strike the brawler from 4,7 at 77 percent, or ride round to 5,6, behind it with Wren on its far side, at 93. He rode, took the counter to 4 HP, and then had one tile of Canto: go, or stay and be the body behind the brawler for Wren's strike. He stayed, Wren finished it at 97 pinned, Teodor and Ottilie killed the archer, and the whole field group was dead on turn 3.
Notes: Turn 2 I set a two-deep block (Teodor 3,5 and Wren 3,6 in front, Pell and the captain behind) so that no enemy could stand behind a front unit; the price was Wren in reach at 20 against 20, and the brawler put her on 6. That formation question was the pincer's too: with Wren back on 2,6, Teodor's flank opened and his line went to 25 against 21. On turn 3 my first line sent Wren at the brawler before Ansgar, and she died on its double counter; I spent the Recall. Honesty about turn 3: after the Recall I knew Wren's roll landed, so her pin decided nothing. Ansgar's ride did. Turn 6 Ottilie doubled the hexer from 12,6 and the Reeve woke; on 6 HP she could not be covered, because his Toll Spear reached her from 14,6, and it killed her at 63 percent. Turn 7 the Reeve stood on 14,6 with Teodor beside him, which made him pinnable from three sides: Pell's Cinder (11), Wren from 14,5 (6 x2), and then the captain from 14,7 read 98 percent with Wren behind him; Teodor finished him at 79 without it. What was not surprising: the enemy never pinned anyone. The planner prices a pin that stands but never makes one, so every pin in the game was mine. What to watch: whether a cold chair moves a unit for a pin without being told the rule exists, and whether the enemy half is worth an arm.

## 2026-09-27 — Brackwater Cut in daylight with the pincer and the enemy's anvils (issue 429) — Code

Seed: 443 on `docs/samples/brackwater_cut_pincer.map` (the shipped Brackwater, `pincer: on`, no `dusk`). Result: lost on enemy phase 6 of 8, all three Recalls spent, all five fell, nobody escaped.
Warm: I built the anvil arm and have played the shipped Brackwater at dusk several times. This is my first daylight Brackwater since 0074. All three Recalls are disclosed below. The first undid a blunder in plain sight (Rook ended in the fort archer's reach and was one-shot). The second, on turn 4, was informed: I had seen Dunstan's 77 miss. The third undid a scripting slip: I queued `end` behind a `threat` that read 24 against 14 and never read it.
Tension: 7/10   Choice: 5/10   Surprise: 7/10
Best turn: turn 4, the second time. With the chase through the door, I stacked Dunstan at 13,1, Rook 14,1, Wren 14,2, the captain 15,0 and Pell 16,1. Every tile behind a unit was either mine or out of reach, and Dunstan's worst case was 12 against 14. He lived. Pell didn't: the archer's shot on Wren woke the bank by noise, and the shieldbearer walked north and killed her. `threat` had listed the bank as able to strike Pell if woken.
Notes: Denials by the round 91 rule: none counted. The turn 4 stack sealed every pin line, but I chose it for the threat totals, not for the pins, and Dunstan could not have gone further anyway. So it was a formation I would have walked into regardless, and it counts for nothing. The arm itself fired, which Sallow never managed. On enemy phase 5 two enemies acted ahead of id order as anvils. Rider-1 stepped to 13,0 beside Rook, but that pin went unused because the archer shot Rook dead. The brawler stepped to 14,3 beside Wren, and rider-2 killed her from 14,1 at +15. The captain on 18,0 was then struck by the soldier from 19,0, pinned by the shieldbearer on 17,0. That pin is the finding. `threat captain from 18,0` had listed four enemies, all striking from 18,1, and nothing from 17,0 or 19,0. So I could not have denied a pin I was not shown. The kill clause needs that question answered before Chat's cold play (PR Unsure). The door at 11,3 is the only ground passage, and a unit on it cannot be pinned from the west, so the arm only reaches you after you leave the door. On an Escape map you have to leave it, which is the right shape. Dunstan's death on turn 5 was mine: a 77 with a 73 counter at 5 HP, when Wren could have taken the 1-HP brigand for free. Moment I stopped caring: turn 6, the captain alone at 7 HP with the hexer's 98 reaching every tile he could stand on.

## 2026-09-27 — Saltmarsh Ford with the brace (13.14 keep round, issue 430) — Code

Seed: 449 on `docs/samples/saltmarsh_ford_brace.map` (the shipped Saltmarsh Ford with `brace: on`). Result: won on turn 13 of 18, no Recall spent, Teodor dead.
Warm: I built the rule, and this is my fifth Saltmarsh. The Recall browser was opened once in a scratch run on turn 12: a Recall to turn 11 replayed the same rolls, so Teodor died either way, and I kept the game as played. Chat's #131 re-rate of the plain map, the baseline this round reads against, is not in yet.
Tension: 6/10   Choice: 7/10   Surprise: 5/10
Best turn: turn 5. To wake the fort, Teodor stepped onto 10,5, and `threat` showed the archer and the wingrider able to reach either him or Wren on 8,5. Unbraced, Wren read 70 and 65. Braced, she read 43 and 37. Wren had a forward move, and she waited instead. Both strikes went to Teodor, who had the counter for the wingrider. On turn 6 Ottilie's bow killed the wingrider at 77.
Notes: The round's two measures. The leader dies on turn 13. Player turns spent on him: five, turns 9 to 13. Turns with no forecast above 50: one, turn 9, when he sat braced and read 47 for the captain and 39 for Ottilie. That turn I sent Ottilie into his Toll Axe's range 2 as bait. The attack was a 39 she was never going to rely on; the step was the point. He answered on the enemy phase, and his brace was gone for the rest of the game. That was a deliberate bait, of a boss, not a hold. On this map the leader braces only on an enemy phase with nobody in his reach. Once any unit stands within 2 of the fort he strikes, so after turn 9 he never braced again, and my forecasts on him read 60 to 73. The long part came after that, and brace had nothing to do with it: the fort heals 5 a turn against about 7 a turn of expected damage, and his Steel Axe one-shots a wounded unit. Turn 11 I put Teodor on 10,1 at 12 HP without reading `threat`, and a 74 for 16 killed him. That was my blunder, not the rule's. Hold-bait signal: none possible here. The ford pair is aggressive and never waits. The fort guards slept, and a sleeper never braces. Once awake they moved every phase. Nothing on Saltmarsh is a hold, so the signal stays with the next map that has one. Not tense: turns 2 to 4, the ford pair walking into the captain in the forest, same as the plain map. Moment I stopped caring: none; the leader at 6 HP with Wren's 63 as the last swing was a real coin.
